</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Freshwater macrophytes shape not only the morphological “architecture” of shallow-water ecosystems but also their chemical milieu via low-molecular-weight organic compounds (LMWOCs) that may regulate phytoplankton, periphyton, and the microbiome within the leaf/shoot diffusive boundary layer and the surrounding water column. In this study, GC–MS (gas chromatography–mass spectrometry) was used to identify major LMWOCs of the low-molecular-weight metabolome (LMWM) in 11 widely distributed macrophyte species (<em>Myriophyllum spicatum</em> L., <em>Sparganium emersum</em> Rehm., <em>Sparganium gramineum</em> Georgi, the hybrid <em>Sparganium</em> × <em>foliosum</em> A. A. Bobrov, Volkova, Mochalova et Chemeris, <em>Persicaria amphibia</em> (L.) Delarbre, <em>Potamogeton perfoliatus</em> L., <em>Nuphar lutea</em> (L.) Sibth. &amp; Sm., <em>Potamogeton pectinatus</em> L., <em>Potamogeton natans</em> L., <em>Lobelia dortmanna</em> L., and <em>Ceratophyllum demersum</em> L.). Compounds contributing more than 1% to the total LMWOCs pool were considered major, increasing the ecological realism of interpretations by focusing on metabolites more likely to reach effective concentrations in the plant microenvironment. For interspecific comparisons, the maximum recorded values of relative abundance and concentrations were used to estimate species “potential”. In total, 137 major LMWOCs were detected (four remained unidentified), and their numbers varied markedly among taxa (from 11 in <em>N. lutea</em> to 71 in <em>P. perfoliatus</em>). Similarity analyses (Jaccard, Sørensen–Czekanowski, Morisita–Horn) indicated that similarity based on compound lists and similarity based on dominance structure may diverge, reflecting differences between the “LMWOCs set” and the quantitative architecture of LMWOCs within the LMWM. Fatty acids formed the core of the major fraction in all species: they were among the top three compounds in all 11 macrophytes and ranked first or second in 10 of 11, highlighting the lipid module as a universal “structure–signaling–defense/allelopathy” hub in aquatic plants. Also, an analysis of the ecological-biochemical role of the main major LMWOCs in the studied aquatic macrophytes is presented. Overall, the data offer a comparable, ecologically oriented framework for interpreting chemical regulation of communities in macrophyte-dominated habitats and for selecting target compounds/species for subsequent bioassay and field studies.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> freshwater macrophytes, low-molecular-weight metabolome, major low-molecular-weight organic compounds, GC–MS, allelopathy, fatty acids, periphyton, phytoplankton, similarity indices (Jaccard, Sørensen–Czekanowski, Morisita–Horn)</p></section></section><section id="sec1-molecules-31-00895"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Freshwater aquatic macrophytes are among the key habitat-forming components of aquatic ecosystems, shaping the spatial structure and functioning of shallow waterbodies and the littoral zones of large lakes. In ponds, oxbows and slowly flowing river reaches, canals, wetlands, and shallow lake areas (including the littoral), macrophyte stands create most of the structural (three-dimensional) complexity of habitats. By affecting hydrodynamics, the underwater light climate, suspended-matter retention, and the availability of surfaces for colonization, they ultimately determine the distribution and productivity of aquatic biota [<a href="#B1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-molecules-31-00895">1</a>,<a href="#B2-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-molecules-31-00895">2</a>,<a href="#B3-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-molecules-31-00895">3</a>]. These effects are regarded as one of the central mechanisms maintaining functional heterogeneity in shallow systems, enhancing resistance to eutrophication, and stabilizing a “clear-water” ecosystem state by competing with phytoplankton for resources [<a href="#B3-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-molecules-31-00895">3</a>,<a href="#B4-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-molecules-31-00895">4</a>,<a href="#B5-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-molecules-31-00895">5</a>].</p>
<p>Alongside morphological habitat engineering, macrophytes also structure freshwater systems biochemically, influencing both the water column and the near-bottom layer. Of particular ecological interest are low-molecular-weight organic compounds (LMWOCs), such as phenolics, terpenoids, fatty acids, and other metabolites. These LMWOCs can alter the growth, physiology, and community structure of phytoplankton, epiphyton/periphyton, and the associated microbiome. This chemical regulation involves the synthesis and release of compounds that may act as defensive metabolites, antimicrobial agents, infochemicals, and allelochemicals affecting competing primary producers and associated biota [<a href="#B6-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-molecules-31-00895">6</a>,<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B8-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-molecules-31-00895">8</a>]. In the literature, such regulation is commonly framed as a combination of direct allelopathic effects and indirect pathways. The latter include changes in the resource environment, shifts in selective pressures on taxa, and modifications of trophic and microbial interactions. The contribution of macrophytes to the stability of the “clear-water” state in shallow lakes is often interpreted as the outcome of several simultaneous mechanisms, among which allelopathy is considered biogeochemically significant [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B9-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-molecules-31-00895">9</a>,<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>]. Moreover, it has been reported that allelopathy caused stronger inhibition than the shading effect and nutrient competition and dominated the combined mechanisms [<a href="#B11-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-molecules-31-00895">11</a>].</p>
<p>If even a small fraction of the thousands of chemicals produced by different plant species (aquatic and terrestrial) affects their neighbors, then species-specific interactions, natural selection, coevolution, and community functioning may deviate substantially from predictions of conceptual models based solely on resource-competition theory [<a href="#B12-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-molecules-31-00895">12</a>]. For submerged macrophytes, the release of compounds with pronounced algicidal activity has been documented (including hydrolysable polyphenols in <em>Myriophyllum spicatum</em>), and this has been linked to the suppression of specific phytoplankton groups and/or shifts in competitive outcomes in the water column [<a href="#B13-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-molecules-31-00895">13</a>]. Experimental studies also show that macrophyte chemical effects may target not only phytoplankton [<a href="#B14-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-molecules-31-00895">14</a>] but also epiphytic communities, reducing fouling on leaf and shoot surfaces and thereby influencing light availability and the photosynthetic performance of the plants themselves [<a href="#B15-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-molecules-31-00895">15</a>,<a href="#B16-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-molecules-31-00895">16</a>]. Inputs of LMWOCs may additionally occur via autolysis/leaching from submerged macrophyte tissues [<a href="#B17-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-molecules-31-00895">17</a>], underscoring the role of macrophyte stands as a continuous source of organic “fuel” and chemical signals (including allelopathic impact) for microbial and algal communities.</p>
<p>A special role in these biochemical interactions is played by the diffusive (boundary) layer at the surface of leaves and shoots, where limited exchange with bulk water generates the steepest microgradients of oxygen, pH, and dissolved substances. The existence of diffusive boundary layers (DBL)—and their dependence on flow velocity, irradiance, and surface roughness—defines the spatial scale of the chemical microenvironment in which direct contacts occur among macrophytes and epiphyton/periphyton, the microbiome, and phytoplankton. Hydrodynamics and biofouling structure can alter oxygen profiles and processes at the leaf–biofilm interface, thereby modifying conditions for microorganisms and epiphytic algae, affecting macrophyte growth, and potentially changing the effectiveness of chemical regulation mediated by macrophyte LMWOCs [<a href="#B8-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-molecules-31-00895">8</a>,<a href="#B18-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-molecules-31-00895">18</a>,<a href="#B19-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-molecules-31-00895">19</a>,<a href="#B20-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-molecules-31-00895">20</a>]. Therefore, the ecological “effect” of macrophyte metabolites should be considered not only as a function of their presence in the water but also as the outcome of transport and transformation within the boundary-layer microenvironment of leaves and shoots.</p>
<p>Recent microbiome research further indicates that aquatic plants and their associated microbial communities function as an integrated system in which microbial metabolism can modify the plant’s chemical profile (and thus its metabolites) and, in turn, influence ecological interactions [<a href="#B21-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-molecules-31-00895">21</a>]. Particularly important is the concept of the aquatic-plant exometabolome—the set of LMWOCs that actually enter the external environment, forming an external LMWOCs pool and participating in interorganism interactions. For submerged plants, diverse exudate components have been described, and both the composition and intensity of exometabolite release depend on plant species, growth stage, and environmental factors (light, temperature, trophic status, etc.) [<a href="#B22-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-molecules-31-00895">22</a>]. Importantly, the exometabolome is not chemically “static”: its composition and activity are determined not only by plant biosynthesis but also by subsequent transformation in the water and within biofilms.</p>
<p>Accordingly, the ecological effect of the LMWOCs is governed by the dynamics of at least three processes: (i) release/input into the DBL and bulk water, (ii) physicochemical partitioning and transformation across microgradients, and (iii) biotic transformation by the microbiome [<a href="#B18-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-molecules-31-00895">18</a>,<a href="#B19-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-molecules-31-00895">19</a>,<a href="#B23-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-molecules-31-00895">23</a>,<a href="#B24-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-molecules-31-00895">24</a>].</p>
<p>In metabolomics, the metabolome is viewed as the ensemble of small molecules reflecting genetic and environmental regulation, and plant LMWOCs profiles obtained by mass-spectrometric approaches are characterized by broad chemical diversity [<a href="#B25-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-molecules-31-00895">25</a>]. Contemporary metabolomics emphasizes that the metabolome—including the low-molecular-weight metabolome (LMWM)—represents an integrated response of an organism to genotype and environment, while analytical platforms (including gas chromatography–mass spectrometry (GC–MS)) enable the detection of numerous metabolites and the comparison of profiles across organisms and habitats [<a href="#B25-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-molecules-31-00895">25</a>,<a href="#B26-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-molecules-31-00895">26</a>]. Studies in aquatic metabolomics indicate that plant LMWMs comprise tens, hundreds, and even thousands of LMWOCs from different functional chemical classes [<a href="#B27-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-molecules-31-00895">27</a>,<a href="#B28-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-molecules-31-00895">28</a>,<a href="#B29-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-molecules-31-00895">29</a>,<a href="#B30-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-molecules-31-00895">30</a>,<a href="#B31-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-molecules-31-00895">31</a>], and GC–MS profiling allows large metabolite sets to be identified and compared, capturing genetic and environmental differences [<a href="#B32-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-molecules-31-00895">32</a>,<a href="#B33-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-molecules-31-00895">33</a>]. For aquatic macrophytes inhabiting highly heterogeneous environments (gradients of light, flow, mineralization, trophic state, and fouling), such chemical multicomponent complexity provides a basis for fine-scale tuning of plant interactions with aquatic communities.</p>
<p>Clearly, it is impossible for a single study to elucidate the ecological and biochemical functions of the entire set of compounds in macrophyte LMWMs in aquatic ecosystems. Nevertheless, from an ecological perspective, the most likely “drivers” of community-level impacts are the major components of the LMWM, because they account for the largest share of the total LMWOCs pool and thus have the greatest chance to reach effective concentrations in the microenvironment at the plant surface as well as in the water. In the present study, “major components” are defined as compounds contributing more than 1% of the total sum of LMWOCs concentrations, calculated from peak areas in the total ion chromatogram (TIC) [<a href="#B34-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-molecules-31-00895">34</a>,<a href="#B35-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-molecules-31-00895">35</a>,<a href="#B36-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-molecules-31-00895">36</a>]. This focus is methodologically justified for two reasons. First, major components are the most likely to generate measurable organic fluxes and to reach concentrations comparable to biological response thresholds within the boundary layer and the water column [<a href="#B18-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-molecules-31-00895">18</a>,<a href="#B19-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-molecules-31-00895">19</a>,<a href="#B37-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-molecules-31-00895">37</a>]. Second, for many allelopathic systems, a key challenge is the “gap” between effective doses observed in laboratory experiments and real concentrations in nature [<a href="#B12-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-molecules-31-00895">12</a>,<a href="#B38-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-molecules-31-00895">38</a>,<a href="#B39-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-molecules-31-00895">39</a>]. Therefore, prioritizing major compounds increases the ecological realism of interpretations and facilitates translation from chemical profiles to community-level effects.</p>
<p>At the same time, when focusing only on major components, it is essential to recognize that minor metabolites can be highly bioactive at low concentrations [<a href="#B40-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-molecules-31-00895">40</a>,<a href="#B41-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-molecules-31-00895">41</a>,<a href="#B42-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-molecules-31-00895">42</a>]. “Majority” increases the likelihood of ecologically relevant concentrations, but it does not guarantee maximal biological activity. Thus, detailed, targeted studies of the ecological role of particular plant species in aquatic communities should also consider highly bioactive minor components of the LMWM.</p>
<p>Despite the growing number of studies addressing individual allelochemicals in individual macrophyte taxa, directly comparable interspecific datasets on the major fraction of the LMWM, generated within a single study and interpreted in an ecological context, remain limited. Therefore, to our knowledge, the present work provides the first systematic comparison of major LMWOCs across 11 widespread freshwater macrophyte species, integrating metabolomic analysis with an ecological–functional approach and establishing a necessary basis for prioritizing target compounds and macrophyte species for subsequent research in ecological metabolomics.</p>
<p>Given the above, the aims of this work were (i) to identify the major components of the LMWM in widely distributed freshwater macrophytes (<em>Myriophyllum spicatum</em> L., <em>Sparganium emersum</em> Rehm., <em>Sparganium gramineum</em> Georgi, the hybrid <em>Sparganium</em> × <em>foliosum</em> A. A. Bobrov, Volkova, Mochalova et Chemeris, <em>Persicaria amphibia</em> (L.) Delarbre, <em>Potamogeton perfoliatus</em> L., <em>Nuphar lutea</em> (L.) Sibth. &amp; Sm., <em>Potamogeton pectinatus</em> L., <em>Potamogeton natans</em> L., <em>Lobelia dortmanna</em> L., <em>Ceratophyllum demersum</em> L.); and (ii) to analyze their ecological and biochemical roles in aquatic ecosystems, with particular emphasis on potential allelopathic interactions of macrophytes with phytoplankton and periphyton (epiphytes). All of the listed species typically form well-developed plant associations in characteristic habitats and thus provide a representative model for discussing how major macrophyte metabolites may participate in the chemical regulation of community structure and productivity in shallow freshwater ecosystems.</p></section><section id="sec2-molecules-31-00895"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Results and Discussion</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-molecules-31-00895"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. General Characterization of the Major-Component Composition of the Studied Species</h3>
<p>In total, 137 LMWOCs were identified as major compounds across the 11 studied species, because they occurred at concentrations &gt;1% of the sum of all LMWOCs. Four of these major components remained unidentified. The number of major LMWOCs varied widely among the studied taxa, ranging from 11 compounds in <em>N. lutea</em> to 71 LMWOCs in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="molecules-31-00895-t001"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/molecules-31-00895-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn2"><p>Note: %—percentage content of a group of LMWOCs from the sum of all LMWOCs; C—absolute content of a group of LMWOCs, μg·g<sup>−1</sup> DW; <em>n</em>—number of major LMWOCs in a group in a specific sample; “<em>n</em>” is an integer value for each sample; where <em>n</em> is shown as 0 while CV is non-zero, this indicates that the mean value of <em>n</em> (averaged over several samples) was &lt;0.5 and therefore rounded to 0 in the table; N—total number of major compounds for a species; Mean—arithmetic mean; SEM—standard error of the mean; CV—coefficient of variation; «–»—the calculation of the indicator is not applicable.</p></div></div></section><p>Only three compounds were major components in all 11 macrophyte species, namely the fatty acids tetradecanoic acid (myristic acid) and hexadecanoic acid (palmitic acid), and the alkane tricosane (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). Two additional LMWOCs were major components in 10 out of 11 species: (E,7R,11R)-3,7,11,15-tetramethylhexadec-2-en-1-ol (phytol) and (9Z,12Z)-octadeca-9,12-dienoic acid (linoleic acid) (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). When considering the distribution of major components among chemical classes in the studied species, the following characteristic patterns can be highlighted.</p>
<section id="sec2dot1dot1-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.1. Species of the Genus <em>Sparganium</em>
</h4>
<p>The distribution of major components in the studied parental species <em>S. gramineum</em> and <em>S. emersum</em>, as well as in their hybrid <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em>, is presented in <a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>. The highest number of major LMWOCs (25) was found in the <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> hybrid, and the lowest (13) in <em>S. emersum</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). For <em>S. gramineum</em>, 20 major LMWOCs were noted.</p>
<p>Both the parental species and the hybrid showed a predominance of hydrocarbons among the major components of the LMWM (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Subdominant groups differed: in <em>S. emersum</em> and <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em>, the largest contributions after hydrocarbons were made by alcohols and carboxylic acids, whereas in <em>S. gramineum</em> they were carboxylic acids and aldehydes (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<p>Overall, the analysis of the LMWM of <em>Sparganium</em> taxa revealed pronounced interspecific differences both in the relative distribution of major LMWOCs across chemical groups and in their number and absolute abundance (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<p>For <em>S. emersum</em>, characterized by a broad ecological amplitude, the most conservative elements of LMWM organization were the dominant fractions (primarily hydrocarbons and alcohols), as reflected by the relatively low variability of their shares. At the same time, the species’ ecological plasticity appeared to be realized mainly through “plastic modules”—carboxylic acids and, to a lesser extent, ketones—for which increased variability was observed (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Similarly, the absolute content of major LMWOCs in this species exhibited lower variability than in the other two taxa (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), indicating a tightly regulated, conservative metabolome organization. Such a combination of a stable metabolomic core with variable functional groups may be regarded as one mechanism supporting ecological resilience in aquatic plants with high tolerance to changing environmental conditions [<a href="#B43-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-molecules-31-00895">43</a>,<a href="#B44-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-molecules-31-00895">44</a>,<a href="#B45-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-molecules-31-00895">45</a>].</p>
<p>In <em>S. gramineum</em> (an oligosaprobic species), the LMWM was characterized by the maintenance of a conservative core due to the dominant hydrocarbon fraction (low variability), alongside distinct within-group differentiation of the remaining components. At the same time, acids, aldehydes, alcohols, and especially sulfur-containing compounds displayed high to extremely high variability (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), pointing to their regulatory and ecologically adaptive roles. Consequently, the metabolomic plasticity of this species may be associated with dynamics of specific functional groups while maintaining an overall metabolic balance. The observed variability patterns of major LMWM components may indicate a greater sensitivity of its metabolism to environmental conditions (the trophic status of the waterbody). Elevated metabolic sensitivity of specialized taxa to environmental change has been reported in studies of plant metabolomic regulation [<a href="#B46-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-molecules-31-00895">46</a>,<a href="#B47-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-molecules-31-00895">47</a>].</p>
<p>The hybrid <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em>, inhabiting a wide trophic range (from oligotrophic to eutrophic lakes), occupied an intermediate yet non-additive position relative to the parental forms. The total number of major components in the hybrid was markedly higher than in the parental taxa, and they were distributed across six chemical groups, whereas in the parental forms only five (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Thus, the LMWM of <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> combines a stable structural core with pronounced hypervariability of individual functional groups (sulfur-containing compounds, ketones, and carboxylic acids) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), indicating non-additive, hierarchically more complex regulation of metabolism in the hybrid.</p>
<p>It has been shown [<a href="#B48-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-molecules-31-00895">48</a>] that hybridization often leads to niche expansion via non-additive phenotypic and physiological effects. This agrees with our results on the variability of major LMWM components, which reflect the hybrid’s capacity to occur across the full trophic spectrum of lakes (from oligotrophic to eutrophic types) combined with a high quantitative level of metabolism. It has also been demonstrated that increased metabolite accumulation in hybrids is linked to non-additive regulation of metabolic pathways [<a href="#B47-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-molecules-31-00895">47</a>]. The results obtained allow us to hypothesize qualitatively more complex regulation of individual metabolite groups in <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em>, which is supported by findings from [<a href="#B49-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-molecules-31-00895">49</a>] showing that hybrids are characterized by non-additive and “reconfigured” metabolic pathways. In addition, hybrids may exhibit increased variability of specific metabolic modules while maintaining moderate stability of the overall metabolome [<a href="#B50-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-molecules-31-00895">50</a>], consistent with the combination of stable structure and high variability of particular groups in <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em>. The extremely high variability of sulfur-containing compounds and ketones in <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> is further supported by documented transgressive (exceeding the parental range) metabolite variation in hybrids [<a href="#B51-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-molecules-31-00895">51</a>].</p>
<p>From a broader evolutionary perspective, the metabolomic patterns observed in the parental taxa <em>S. gramineum</em> and <em>S. emersum</em> and their hybrid <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> may reflect both phylogenetic constraints and adaptive/ecological modulation. The presence of a stable ‘structural core’ in <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> is consistent with a phylogenetic signal, reflecting shared biosynthetic capacities inherited from the parental species. In contrast, the non-additive and transgressive variability of specific functional groups suggests a hybrid-specific regulatory reconfiguration, which may increase ecological plasticity, for example, along trophic gradients. Notably, disentangling phylogenetic signal from habitat-driven effects would require dedicated phylogenetic comparative studies and broader sampling, which is beyond the scope of the present study.</p>
<p>Thus, the composition of major LMWOCs in the hybrid <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> supports an ecological interpretation of this hybrid as a form with enhanced adaptive capacity, reflecting the general tendency toward the wide occurrence of hybrid <em>Sparganium</em> forms in diverse aquatic habitats and their ecological significance [<a href="#B44-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-molecules-31-00895">44</a>,<a href="#B45-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-molecules-31-00895">45</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot1dot2-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.2. Species of the Genus <em>Potamogeton</em>
</h4>
<p>The composition of major LMWOCs in the LMWM was most diverse in the <em>Potamogeton</em> species (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>; <a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). The total number of identified major LMWOCs was similar (36 and 31) in <em>P. pectinatus</em> and <em>P. natans</em>, whereas it was maximal (71) in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> among all studied macrophyte species.</p>
<p>The number of major LMWOCs per sample (<em>n</em>) in the <em>Potamogeton</em> species exhibited low variability (from CV = 0.11 in <em>P. natans</em> to 0.19 in <em>P. perfoliatus</em>) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). The relative contribution of major LMWOCs to the total LMWOCs pool also showed very low to low variability (CV = 0.05–0.12) across all three species, whereas the absolute concentrations of major LMWOCs varied strongly (CV = 0.56–1.04) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<p>According to the obtained data (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), the LMWM of <em>P. pectinatus</em> is characterized by: (i) the dominance of primary-metabolism classes (carboxylic acids, alcohols, ketones), (ii) a high share of oxygen-containing classes among major compounds, and (iii) a clear separation between a conservative “metabolic core” (carboxylic acids, alcohols, ketones)—where alcohols and ketones show the lowest variability—and an ecologically plastic periphery, i.e., groups with very high variability (phenols, aldehydes, and diverse functional groups).</p>
<p>The LMWM of <em>P. natans</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) fundamentally differs from that of <em>P. pectinatus</em> and is shifted from an acid-oriented to a lipid/ester-oriented metabolome type. This is supported by: (i) a high proportion of esters and alcohols, indicating enhanced lipophilic surface fractions (including wax esters and long-chain alcohols) that shape barrier and interfacial properties of the leaf surface [<a href="#B52-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-molecules-31-00895">52</a>,<a href="#B53-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-molecules-31-00895">53</a>], and also pointing to active chemical interactions with the environment [<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B54-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-molecules-31-00895">54</a>]; (ii) a broader spectrum of compounds classified as diverse functional groups, which may be typical for floating-leaved species [<a href="#B55-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-molecules-31-00895">55</a>,<a href="#B56-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-molecules-31-00895">56</a>] inhabiting conditions of variable light and hydrodynamics [<a href="#B57-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-molecules-31-00895">57</a>]; and (iii) high variability of carboxylic acids, reflecting dependence on environmental conditions (light, trophic state, CO<sub>2</sub>, etc.) [<a href="#B58-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-molecules-31-00895">58</a>,<a href="#B59-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-molecules-31-00895">59</a>]. In contrast to <em>P. pectinatus</em>, which exhibits a conservative, “primary” metabolome, <em>P. natans</em> demonstrates a metabolomic strategy of ecological plasticity close to an adaptive/signaling type [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B54-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-molecules-31-00895">54</a>,<a href="#B60-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-molecules-31-00895">60</a>].</p>
<p>The LMWM of <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) appears to reflect a moderately plastic, but not extreme, strategy that is intermediate between the conservative type (<em>P. pectinatus</em>) and the ecological/signaling type (<em>P. natans</em>). This is suggested by key features such as: the absence of a clearly dominant chemical group; an increased contribution of oxidative and stress-associated fractions (aldehydes, ketones); a minimal contribution of phenolic and aromatic compounds; and overall very high variability of absolute concentrations, indicating sensitivity to environmental conditions. As a typical submerged macrophyte with a well-developed leaf apparatus, <em>P. perfoliatus</em> exhibits a LMWM oriented toward balancing primary metabolism and ecological adaptation, including allelopathic regulation of phytoplankton development [<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B54-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-molecules-31-00895">54</a>].</p>
<p>A comparison of the metabolomic profiles of the three <em>Potamogeton</em> species demonstrates a clear link between the chemical organization of the LMWM, the morpho-ecological growth form, and the potential role of macrophytes in allelopathic interactions with phytoplankton and epiphytes. These results are consistent with the concept of species specificity and the context dependence of allelopathy in aquatic plants [<a href="#B60-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-molecules-31-00895">60</a>].</p>
<p>The floating-leaved species <em>P. natans</em> is characterized by a LMWM with an increased proportion of lipophilic and ester compounds, which is consistent with reports on specialized antialgal furanoditerpenes in this species capable of suppressing the growth of microalgae [<a href="#B56-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-molecules-31-00895">56</a>]. Experimental studies also show that floating-leaved macrophytes can, in some cases, inhibit cyanobacteria more effectively than fully submerged forms, supporting the interpretation of <em>P. natans</em> as a chemically active regulator of their abundance [<a href="#B61-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-molecules-31-00895">61</a>].</p>
<p>The fully submerged species <em>P. pectinatus</em> and <em>P. perfoliatus</em> demonstrate more conservative metabolomic profiles. Nevertheless, submerged macrophytes are also known to exert allelopathic inhibitory effects on phytoplankton [<a href="#B54-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-molecules-31-00895">54</a>], and the antialgal activity of their exudates is often associated with lipophilic fractions [<a href="#B62-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-molecules-31-00895">62</a>]. For <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, interactions with epiphytes are particularly important: epiphytic overgrowth substantially affects the photobiological characteristics and plant production, making chemical regulation of epiphytes functionally significant [<a href="#B63-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-molecules-31-00895">63</a>,<a href="#B64-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-molecules-31-00895">64</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot1dot3-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.3. <em>Myriophyllum spicatum</em>
</h4>
<p>A total of 25 compounds were identified in the major LMWOCs fraction of <em>M. spicatum</em>, with carboxylic acids and hydrocarbons dominating both in terms of cumulative relative contribution and absolute concentration (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>; <a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). The mean cumulative share of the accounted groups was high and exhibited very low variability (82.01% of total LMWOCs, CV = 0.04). In contrast, the total concentration of major LMWOCs was characterized by very high variability (CV = 0.69). On average, 17 major LMWOCs were identified per sample (CV = 0.23), indicating the presence of a relatively stable qualitative “core” of the major fraction despite pronounced variability in the contributions of individual chemical classes.</p>
<p>The major LMWOCs of the LMWM of <em>M. spicatum</em> are characterized by a marked lipid–hydrophobic dominance, with carboxylic acids and hydrocarbons playing a key role. The predominance of carboxylic acids, both in relative contribution and concentration, indicates a substantial role of the fatty acid pool in shaping the chemical influence of <em>M. spicatum</em> on the surrounding aquatic environment. For <em>M. spicatum</em>, it has been demonstrated that released fatty acids exhibit anti-cyanobacterial activity and may directly contribute to allelopathic effects in aquatic ecosystems [<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>,<a href="#B66-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-molecules-31-00895">66</a>]. The prominent variability in fatty acid concentrations, combined with more moderate variability in their relative contribution, reflects the dual role of lipids as structural components and as participants in dynamic physiological processes within the plant itself, as well as in biochemical interactions within the aquatic ecosystem (e.g., direct chemical effects on phytoplankton and microbiota).</p>
<p>The substantial contribution of hydrocarbons highlights the importance of hydrophobic components in the major fraction. The high proportion of alkanes and their low variability, together with the limited variability in the number of alkanes among samples, may be related both to relatively conservative biosynthetic control of these compounds and to their sorption and biotransformation on plant surfaces and by epiphytic biota [<a href="#B52-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-molecules-31-00895">52</a>].</p>
<p>Alcohols form a relatively stable supporting class, whereas aldehydes and ketones exhibit increased variability, which is consistent with their role as products of oxylipin and oxidative pathways activated under damage or stress conditions [<a href="#B67-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-molecules-31-00895">67</a>,<a href="#B68-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-molecules-31-00895">68</a>]. These classes represent the “reactive” part of the LMWM, which is sensitive to environmental conditions and largely responsible for plant adaptation to specific habitats.</p>
<p>Overall, the profile of major components in <em>M. spicatum</em> indicates a functional differentiation between a stable lipid–hydrocarbon “framework” and highly variable regulatory compounds. This metabolomic pattern is in good agreement with the well-documented high allelopathic activity of the species, where the overall effect is formed by a combination of, for example, polyphenolic and lipid–acid components [<a href="#B13-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-molecules-31-00895">13</a>,<a href="#B40-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-molecules-31-00895">40</a>,<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>,<a href="#B66-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-molecules-31-00895">66</a>,<a href="#B69-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-molecules-31-00895">69</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot1dot4-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.4. <em>Persicaria amphibia</em>
</h4>
<p>Across the groups of major LMWOCs, <em>P. amphibia</em> exhibited a fatty-acid-dominated structure (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), as carboxylic acids made the largest contribution to the total pool of major components.</p>
<p>The predominance of carboxylic acids (≈53% of the sum of major LMWOCs) and their low relative variability (CV = 0.10) indicate that, in <em>P. amphibia</em>, the lipid fraction forms a “conservative” metabolic framework. Their mean number per sample (6) was also high, with low variability of this metric (CV = 0.20). At the same time, the variability of the absolute content of fatty acids in the LMWM was high (CV = 0.55) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), suggesting that the level of their biosynthesis changes depending on specific aquatic environmental conditions and pointing to a strong regulatory role.</p>
<p>Fatty acids and their derivatives simultaneously perform structural (membranes/cuticular lipids), energetic, and protective functions; many free fatty acids participate in allelopathic interactions, exhibit antimicrobial activity, and may affect the plant-surface microbiome and periphyton [<a href="#B70-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-molecules-31-00895">70</a>,<a href="#B71-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-molecules-31-00895">71</a>]. In addition, unsaturated fatty acids are precursors of oxylipins—a central stress-signaling cascade associated with plant damage, herbivores, and pathogens [<a href="#B72-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-molecules-31-00895">72</a>].</p>
<p>Substantially smaller shares were observed for aldehydes and ketones, with different levels of variability in their relative representation and in the number of compounds in the LMWM—low for aldehydes and very high for ketones (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). High variability was also characteristic of their absolute contents, indicating a close linkage between their synthesis and ambient environmental conditions. Similarly high variability in contents and concentrations was found for alcohols and hydrocarbons (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<p>The remaining groups (aromatic hydrocarbons, esters, sulfur-containing compounds, and unidentified components) formed a set of minor constituents characterized by a high level of variability across all metrics. Overall, major LMWOCs accounted on average for 72.71 ± 2.78% of the total LMWOCs pool, with a very low level of variability (CV = 0.05); on average, 15 ± 1 major compounds were recorded per sample, also with very low variability (CV = 0.07), out of a total pool of N = 21.</p>
<p>The moderate shares of aldehydes and alcohols (together ~9%) are consistent with their role as components of “green leaf volatiles” (GLVs), which are rapidly produced upon damage and are involved in direct and indirect defense (signaling, antimicrobial effects, and mediation of trophic interactions) [<a href="#B73-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-molecules-31-00895">73</a>].</p>
<p>The high variability of ketones and hydrocarbons (CV for proportion = 0.62–0.65) may reflect the plasticity of surface lipids/waxes, which are known to constitute a dynamic barrier responding to abiotic and biotic factors; waxes include alkanes, aldehydes, ketones, and esters and influence stress tolerance and surface colonization [<a href="#B74-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-molecules-31-00895">74</a>,<a href="#B75-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-molecules-31-00895">75</a>]. For the amphibious species <em>P. amphibia</em>, with contrasting aquatic and terrestrial forms, such plasticity is particularly expected and is consistent with the high ecological variability of <em>P. amphibia</em> [<a href="#B76-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-molecules-31-00895">76</a>]. Finally, the broader context of the chemical “landscape” of leaf surfaces supports the interpretation of minor wax-related groups as modules of the plant–environment interface, where small fractions may exert disproportionately important effects [<a href="#B77-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-molecules-31-00895">77</a>]. Although such effects have been documented mainly for terrestrial plants, similar effects can be expected in aquatic macrophytes, especially in amphibious taxa and species with floating leaves.</p></section><section id="sec2dot1dot5-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.5. <em>Nuphar lutea</em>
</h4>
<p>In this study, we analyzed the LMWM of yellow water lily (<em>N. lutea</em>) inhabiting a single lake on the Karelian Isthmus (Northwest Russia) whose trophic status changed among years [<a href="#B78-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-molecules-31-00895">78</a>].</p>
<p>The LMWM of <em>N. lutea</em> was characterized by an extremely strong dominance of carboxylic acids, which accounted on average for 78.67 ± 8.28% of the total pool of major LMWOCs (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Despite low variation in relative abundance (CV = 0.11), the absolute concentrations of carboxylic acids exhibited very high variability (3388.91 ± 2850.72 μg·g<sup>−1</sup> DW; CV = 0.84), indicating substantial fluctuations in the level of their metabolic synthesis, most likely associated with changing environmental conditions in this lake across different years [<a href="#B78-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-molecules-31-00895">78</a>].</p>
<p>The second most important group comprised hydrocarbons, contributing 4.32 ± 1.41% with a relatively high CV (0.33) and moderate absolute concentrations (202.64 ± 103.14 μg·g<sup>−1</sup> DW; CV = 0.51). Aldehydes and esters occurred at minor proportions and were characterized by high variability in both relative abundance and concentrations (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Alcohols were detected only as a single major component (<em>n</em> = 1) and exhibited extremely high variation in concentration (CV = 1.41), highlighting their episodic role within the major LMWOCs pool of this species.</p>
<p>Overall, major LMWOCs constituted most of <em>N. lutea</em> LMWOCs pool (87.32 ± 1.77%) with very low variability (CV = 0.02), while the number of major compounds remained consistently low (9 ± 1; CV = 0.16).</p>
<p>The identified LMWM structure of <em>N. lutea</em> reflects a highly specialized strategy dominated by a limited number of high-concentration compounds, namely fatty acids, which act as precursors of oxylipins/signaling and defense-related molecules and may serve as potential inhibitors of cyanobacteria and other microorganisms [<a href="#B71-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-molecules-31-00895">71</a>,<a href="#B72-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-molecules-31-00895">72</a>,<a href="#B79-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-molecules-31-00895">79</a>].</p>
<p>This pattern is consistent with the notion that the lipid/fatty-acid block in yellow water lily serves both as a “metabolic framework” (membranes, energy metabolism) and as a source of ecologically relevant derivatives capable of affecting epiphyton and phytoplankton [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B60-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-molecules-31-00895">60</a>].</p>
<p>This organization of the major pool differs fundamentally from the more “diversified” profiles obtained for <em>M. spicatum</em> and <em>P. amphibia</em>. A key feature of <em>N. lutea</em> is the discrepancy between the stability of relative composition and the variability of absolute concentrations. This agrees with evidence that, in aquatic macrophytes, the contributions of functional groups and the strength of allelopathy/chemical control of biota are highly species-specific. At the same time, the intensity of allelochemical release/flux and their actual effectiveness in a waterbody are strongly regulated by environmental conditions, microbial degradation, and dilution effects [<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B15-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-molecules-31-00895">15</a>,<a href="#B80-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-molecules-31-00895">80</a>].</p>
<p>The low and stable number of major compounds in <em>N. lutea</em> emphasizes that its defensive/allelopathic potential is more likely realized through quantitative amplification of a limited set of metabolites rather than through chemical diversity. From an ecological perspective, this strategy may enable flexible regulation of chemical pressure on microbial, epiphytic, and phytoplankton communities while maintaining a stable set of “functional” molecules. It has been shown that combinations of polyphenols and fatty acids can suppress cyanobacteria; even if the specific molecules differ, the underlying mechanism—locally high effective concentrations and additive/synergistic effects of allelochemical mixtures—appears to be common across allelopathic systems of aquatic plants [<a href="#B66-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-molecules-31-00895">66</a>,<a href="#B81-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81-molecules-31-00895">81</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot1dot6-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.6. <em>Ceratophyllum demersum</em> and <em>Lobelia dortmanna</em>
</h4>
<p>In both species, “major” LMWOCs constituted the main fraction of the LMWM. However, the structure of the metabolite pools differed markedly.</p>
<p>
<em>C. demersum.</em>
</p>
<p>Major groups accounted for 70.97% of the total LMWOCs pool, with a cumulative concentration of 39.67 μg·g<sup>−1</sup> DW and 26 major compounds (<em>n</em> = 26). The largest contribution was made by ketones, aldehydes, and carboxylic acids, which together comprised more than 50% of the LMWM. Esters, hydrocarbons, and alcohols contributed substantially less to the LMWM of <em>C. demersum</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Overall, the metabolomic profile of <em>C. demersum</em> can be characterized as “multiaxial”, with several co-dominant chemical groups.</p>
<p><em>L. dortmanna</em>.</p>
<p>In this species, major groups comprised 78.57% of the total LMWOCs pool, with a substantially higher cumulative concentration (156.16 μg·g<sup>−1</sup> DW) but a lower number of major compounds (<em>n</em> = 13). The LMWM was strongly dominated by carboxylic acids and hydrocarbons, whereas ketones were of secondary importance. Aldehydes and alcohols were present only at trace levels (~1% each; <em>n</em> = 1), and esters were not detected among the major LMWOCs (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>
<p>Despite the lack of estimates of spatial variability, the major LMWOCs profiles clearly reflect different “dominance strategies” in these two species. In <em>C. demersum</em>, the distribution of the major pool among ketones, aldehydes, and carboxylic acids is typical of active lipid metabolism and oxylipin-related pathways. Free fatty acids serve as substrates for the formation of stress-induced aldehydes and alcohols, as well as other lipid oxidation products, which—together with fatty acids—are involved in signaling, defense, and allelopathic processes [<a href="#B66-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-molecules-31-00895">66</a>,<a href="#B67-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-molecules-31-00895">67</a>,<a href="#B72-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-molecules-31-00895">72</a>]. The substantial contribution of such “reactive” compound classes is consistent with the role of <em>C. demersum</em> as a chemically active component of shallow-water biocenoses, capable of generating locally high concentrations of biologically active metabolites. For this and other submerged macrophytes, inhibitory effects of exometabolites on phytoplankton and cyanobacteria, as well as the role of allelopathy in maintaining clear-water states, have been demonstrated [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B54-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-molecules-31-00895">54</a>].</p>
<p>In <em>L. dortmanna</em>, the dominance of carboxylic acids and hydrocarbons (&gt;71% of the pool; 141.82 μg·g<sup>−1</sup> DW) combined with a low number of major compounds indicates a “structural–barrier” orientation of the LMWM. Long-chain lipids and their derivatives are known to form the basis of hydrophobic plant surface layers, regulating tissue permeability and mechanical stability [<a href="#B53-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-molecules-31-00895">53</a>]. This profile is consistent with the physiology of isoetid plants, which are characterized by restricted leaf gas exchange and a substantial contribution of root-mediated uptake of inorganic carbon from the sediment [<a href="#B82-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82-molecules-31-00895">82</a>,<a href="#B83-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83-molecules-31-00895">83</a>]. Thus, the more “constructive” lipid–barrier metabolomic profile observed in <em>L. dortmanna</em> may represent an adaptive strategy to oligotrophic, soft-water habitats.</p></section></section><section id="sec2dot2-molecules-31-00895"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Similarity Among Species Based on Their Major LMWOCs</h3>
<p><a href="#molecules-31-00895-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> and <a href="#molecules-31-00895-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> present similarity estimates among the studied species based on data on their major LMWOCs, calculated using the Jaccard similarity coefficient (J), the Sørensen–Czekanowski coefficient (Qs), and the Morisita–Horn index (Cmh).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/molecules-31-00895-t003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>Similarity matrices constructed from major LMWOCs using these three indices (Jaccard, Sørensen–Czekanowski, and Morisita–Horn) revealed stable “blocks” of similarity and demonstrated that similarity in composition (presence/absence) and similarity in the structure of dominant components (abundance-weighted) partly diverge. This pattern is expected for metabolomic data, where a relatively small set of compounds may account for most of the total concentration and thus “bring species closer” according to the Morisita–Horn index, even when the overlap in component lists is only moderate [<a href="#B84-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84-molecules-31-00895">84</a>].</p>
<p>The most internally consistent cluster is formed by the genus <em>Sparganium</em> (<em>S. emersum, S. gramineum</em>, and <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em>). According to the J and Qs metrics, <em>Sparganium</em> species exhibit relatively high intragroup similarity, with J = 0.50–0.57 and Qs = 0.67–0.73 between species pairs and the hybrid. Similarity based on the Morisita–Horn index is even higher (Cmh = 0.86–0.96), indicating that not only the set of major LMWOCs but also the dominance structure is highly concordant. Biologically, this is consistent with the notion that closely related taxa often retain similar chemotypes and functional chemical strategies (e.g., defense, antifouling, allelopathy, signaling lipids) [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B85-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B85-molecules-31-00895">85</a>]. The high degree of similarity between the hybrid taxon and its parental species agrees well with evidence that the chemical phenotype of hybrids is often shaped by inheritance and redistribution of parental specialized metabolites, resulting in additive or non-additive, intermediate, or dominant patterns in both compound composition and concentrations [<a href="#B86-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B86-molecules-31-00895">86</a>,<a href="#B87-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B87-molecules-31-00895">87</a>,<a href="#B88-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B88-molecules-31-00895">88</a>,<a href="#B89-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B89-molecules-31-00895">89</a>].</p>
<p>A particularly distinct pair of species is <em>P. amphibia</em> and <em>L. dortmanna</em>, which exhibit the highest quantitative similarity that cannot be explained by taxonomic relatedness. For this pair, very high similarity according to the Morisita–Horn index (Cmh = 0.90) is observed alongside much more moderate compositional overlap (J = 0.26; Qs = 0.41). This combination implies similarity specifically in dominant molecules and indicates convergence at the level of dominant classes or compounds, i.e., leading major components form a similar “mass” of the metabolomic pool, while a substantial part of the major LMWOCs lists remains different. Ecologically, this is consistent with the hypothesis that different hydrophytes may converge toward similar basic strategies: (i) chemical defense and (ii) effects on microbial and algal assemblages through allelopathy, where major LMWOCs present at high concentrations are most likely to reach biologically effective levels [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>]. In our case, fatty acids—represented by palmitic, linoleic, α-linolenic, and other acids—were the dominant components in these species (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>; <a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S1</a>). These LMWOCs may function both as structural membrane lipids and as biologically active molecules involved in allelopathic, stress-related, and defensive responses [<a href="#B67-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-molecules-31-00895">67</a>,<a href="#B71-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-molecules-31-00895">71</a>,<a href="#B73-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-molecules-31-00895">73</a>].</p>
<p>Closely associated with this species pair is <em>N. lutea</em>, which also shows elevated similarity with them, as well as with <em>M. spicatum</em>, according to the Morisita–Horn index (Cmh = 0.69–0.81; <a href="#molecules-31-00895-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>). At the same time, similarity based on J and Qs indices is more moderate (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). This pattern is typical when similar dominant metabolic pathways—primarily lipid and fatty-acid pathways and their derivatives (oxylipins and signaling molecules)—shape a comparable “mass” structure of the LMWM in ecologically different plants [<a href="#B72-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-molecules-31-00895">72</a>,<a href="#B79-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-molecules-31-00895">79</a>,<a href="#B90-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B90-molecules-31-00895">90</a>]. In aquatic macrophytes, fatty acids and their associated products and functions are often considered a key component of chemical defense and interspecific interactions, including effects on phytoplankton and cyanobacteria [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>].</p>
<p><em>M. spicatum</em> exhibits moderate similarity with <em>Sparganium</em> species according to Qs (0.44–0.53) and Cmh (0.47–0.58). This is consistent with the presence of well-documented chemical–ecological mechanisms in <em>M. spicatum</em>, including allelopathically active polyphenols and a range of fatty acids, which may generate a prominent dominance structure and exert strong effects on phytoplankton, including cyanobacteria [<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>,<a href="#B66-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-molecules-31-00895">66</a>]. At the same time, the compositional “tail” of major compounds may differ among genera, thereby reducing J and Qs values.</p>
<p>Within the genus <em>Potamogeton</em>, no single dense cluster of similarity is observed among the studied species, in contrast to what might be expected by analogy with <em>Sparganium</em>. Instead, only moderate similarity occurs between individual species pairs. For example, <em>P. pectinatus</em> and <em>P. natans</em> show Cmh = 0.54 at low J (0.16) and Qs (0.27), indicating relatively high quantitative similarity in dominant components but low similarity in the lists of major LMWOCs. This pattern may reflect the pronounced ecological plasticity of the genus <em>Potamogeton</em> (variation in habitat conditions, seasonality, stressors), under which a conserved “core” of dominant compounds or classes is maintained, whereas the set of accompanying major components varies [<a href="#B29-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-molecules-31-00895">29</a>,<a href="#B91-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B91-molecules-31-00895">91</a>,<a href="#B92-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B92-molecules-31-00895">92</a>]. In contrast, <em>P. perfoliatus</em> generally exhibits lower similarity with most species, indicating a more specific major LMWOCs pool.</p>
<p>In the similarity analysis, <em>C. demersum</em> showed predominantly low J and Qs values with all species (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>), low Cmh values with most species, and relatively higher similarity only with <em>P. pectinatus</em> (Cmh = 0.58; <a href="#molecules-31-00895-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>). This pattern highlights its chemical specificity, possibly related to the absence of a clearly dominant compound group among its major LMWOCs (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Nevertheless, for <em>C. demersum</em>, as for other studied macrophytes, allelopathic activity has been documented in both experimental and field studies [<a href="#B54-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-molecules-31-00895">54</a>,<a href="#B93-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93-molecules-31-00895">93</a>,<a href="#B94-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B94-molecules-31-00895">94</a>,<a href="#B95-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B95-molecules-31-00895">95</a>].</p>
<p>Thus, beyond a purely statistical interpretation, the interspecific similarity matrices (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> and <a href="#molecules-31-00895-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>) can be interpreted in relation to species’ ecological traits. Similarity in the dominance structure of major LMWOCs can be expected among species sharing comparable functional types and habitats, whereas contrasting environmental settings across different waterbody types (e.g., oligotrophic vs. eutrophic systems) may shift metabolite allocation and relative importance, thereby altering dominance patterns in the LMWM. Accordingly, discrepancies between list-based similarity and similarity based on quantitative dominance likely reflect shared eco-biochemical regularities in LMWM formation in aquatic macrophytes, while the specific quantitative architecture of the metabolome is, to a substantial extent, shaped by ecological context.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-molecules-31-00895"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Ecological-Biochemical Role of the Main Major LMWOCs of the Aquatic Macrophytes</h3>
<p><a href="#sec2dot1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Section 2.1</a> and <a href="#sec2dot2-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Section 2.2</a> described the overall structure of the major LMWOCs pool and interspecific differences in the composition and chemical classes of major LMWOCs. In this section, we focus on those compounds that constitute the core of the major pool based on relative contribution and examine the ecological and biochemical roles of the most important major LMWOCs (as well as their chemical groups) identified in the studied macrophytes. These compounds are most likely to determine functional effects in aquatic ecosystems within macrophyte-dominated habitats.</p>
<p>Importantly, when discussing the ecological roles of LMWOCs, it is essential to consider the context of the plant–water interface, where a DBL may generate steep concentration gradients and prolong the residence time of released metabolites relative to the bulk water. This microenvironment constitutes a key locus for interactions with epiphytes and the microbiome and may therefore increase the functional relevance of major LMWOCs even when their concentrations in bulk water are low. Accordingly, we interpret dominant classes of major LMWOCs not only in terms of their biochemical origin but also with respect to their potential to operate within DBL-scale microenvironments.</p>
<p><a href="#molecules-31-00895-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a> presents the top three most abundant major LMWOCs for each studied macrophyte species. Notably, carboxylic acids are included among the top three major LMWOCs in all species, and in 10 out of 11 species, they occupy either the first or second position, or both.</p>
