<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/molecules-31-00895-t004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><section id="sec2dot3dot1-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.1. Fatty Acids</h4>
<p>The predominance of fatty acids—primarily palmitic, linoleic, and α-linolenic acids—indicates the dominance of a lipid metabolic block within the major LMWOCs pool. This pattern is particularly evident in <em>N. lutea</em>, where these three acids together constitute a substantial proportion of the major pool (α-linolenic acid 31.31%, palmitic acid 29.06%, linoleic acid 25.03%). A similar pattern is observed in <em>P. amphibia</em> (palmitic acid 42.53%, α-linolenic acid 10.43%, myristic acid 8.33%), <em>P. pectinatus</em> (palmitic acid 23.92%, myristic acid 14.55%), and <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (α-linolenic acid 18.41%, palmitic acid 13.55%). Functionally, such a “fatty-acid core” is consistent with the well-established role of fatty acids—particularly unsaturated ones—as key precursors of oxylipins involved in stress responses, chemical signaling, antimicrobial and anti-grazer defense, and allelopathic interactions [<a href="#B79-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-molecules-31-00895">79</a>,<a href="#B81-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81-molecules-31-00895">81</a>].</p>
<p>In aquatic macrophytes, fatty acids can be regarded as a functional node linking structure, signaling, and ecological impact, realized in the aquatic environment primarily through bioavailable free fatty acids (FFAs) released during tissue damage, exudation, and detrital decomposition.</p>
<p>From a <em>Metabolome</em>→<em>Defense</em> perspective, the observed dominance of linoleic acid (C18:2) and α-linolenic acid (C18:3) among the top 3 components (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>) is of particular importance. These fatty acids serve as substrates for lipid-mediated signaling and oxylipin/jasmonate-dependent cascades; therefore, the high C18:3/C18:2 maxima observed in <em>N. lutea</em> and <em>P. perfoliatus</em> may be interpreted as reflecting an increased defensive capacity of the lipid module and readiness for stress-induced responses to epiphytic, microbial, and cyanobacterial pressure [<a href="#B96-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B96-molecules-31-00895">96</a>,<a href="#B97-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B97-molecules-31-00895">97</a>].</p>
<p>The transition from <em>Defense/Allelopathy</em>→<em>Periphyton/Phytoplankton</em> is most likely to occur during phases when lipids are converted into FFAs. Experimental studies have demonstrated that FFAs exert cytotoxic effects on algae and cyanobacteria primarily via membrane disruption, including increased permeability, ion leakage, and secondary physiological disturbances, with unsaturated fatty acids generally exhibiting stronger effects than saturated ones [<a href="#B98-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B98-molecules-31-00895">98</a>]. At the same time, in an experimental study [<a href="#B99-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B99-molecules-31-00895">99</a>], it was shown that the effect of nonanoic acid against the cyanobacterium <em>Synechocystis aquatilis</em> Sauvageau (suppression coefficients were more than 20) was more pronounced than that of palmitoleic acid (suppression coefficients were not more than 3.5). In <em>M. spicatum</em>, the release of fatty acids with strong anticyanobacterial activity has been experimentally confirmed [<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>]. In this context, the high fatty-acid contents observed in this and other studied species support the interpretation of species rich in fatty acids—especially unsaturated ones—as potentially lipid-mediated allelopathically active macrophytes influencing phytoplankton and biofilm formation.</p>
<p>At the periphyton level, fatty acids may act as selective allelochemical agents reshaping the bacterial core of biofilms. The antibacterial activity of FFAs is associated with damage to the cellular membrane, disruption of electron transport and oxidative phosphorylation, impairment of cellular energy production, inhibition of enzyme activity, reduced nutrient uptake, formation of peroxidation and auto-oxidation products, or direct bacterial cell lysis [<a href="#B100-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100-molecules-31-00895">100</a>,<a href="#B101-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B101-molecules-31-00895">101</a>]. As a result, both the rate and intensity of surface colonization and the functional structure of epiphytic communities may be altered [<a href="#B100-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100-molecules-31-00895">100</a>]. Importantly, the most realistic ecological scenario involves the action of allelochemical mixtures rather than single compounds, with fatty acids contributing additive or synergistic effects alongside other allelochemicals [<a href="#B102-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B102-molecules-31-00895">102</a>,<a href="#B103-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B103-molecules-31-00895">103</a>,<a href="#B104-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B104-molecules-31-00895">104</a>].</p>
<p>Direct allelopathic effects on algae and cyanobacteria have been demonstrated for α-linolenic, linoleic, palmitoleic, dodecanoic, tetradecanoic, pentadecanoic, hexadecanoic, octadecanoic, cis-6-octadecenoic, cis-9-octadecenoic, heptanoic, octanoic, nonanoic, cis-5,8,11,14,17-eicosapentaenoic, and cis-4,7,10,13,16,19-docosahexaenoic acids [<a href="#B65-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-molecules-31-00895">65</a>,<a href="#B98-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B98-molecules-31-00895">98</a>,<a href="#B99-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B99-molecules-31-00895">99</a>,<a href="#B105-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B105-molecules-31-00895">105</a>,<a href="#B106-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B106-molecules-31-00895">106</a>,<a href="#B107-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B107-molecules-31-00895">107</a>,<a href="#B108-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B108-molecules-31-00895">108</a>,<a href="#B109-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B109-molecules-31-00895">109</a>,<a href="#B110-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B110-molecules-31-00895">110</a>,<a href="#B111-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B111-molecules-31-00895">111</a>,<a href="#B112-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B112-molecules-31-00895">112</a>]. Notably, the first seven acids in this list may occur as major components in the LMWM of the macrophytes examined in the present study (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>).</p>
<p>At the ecosystem level, chemical pressure exerted by FFA-allelochemicals should be regarded as an important component of an integrated mechanism through which macrophytes can reduce phytoplankton competitiveness and maintain clearer-water states. However, this effect is likely achieved not only through allelopathy but also via alternative mechanisms such as light attenuation, competition for nutrients, and reduced nutrient availability for phytoplankton [<a href="#B9-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-molecules-31-00895">9</a>,<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot2-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.2. Alcohols</h4>
<p>According to the maximum relative content (in %) (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>), the alcohol profile in 11 studied macrophyte species is formed by three “cores”: (i) terpenoid alcohols (including labdane-type diterpenes), (ii) chlorophyll-derived alcohols (primarily phytol), and (iii) aliphatic or “green” alcohols (C6–C8 compounds and long-chain fatty alcohols). These groups are potentially associated both with the plants’ intrinsic chemical defense and with epiphytic microbiota and periphyton within the DBL adjacent to plant surfaces.</p>
<p>Among all major alcohols identified, only two compounds—manool and phytol—ranked among the three most abundant components by maximum relative content, and only in three species: <em>M. spicatum</em>, <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, and <em>P. natans</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>).</p>
<p>In aquatic macrophytes, alcohol metabolites play a dual role: (1) as intraplastidial and membrane-associated components and products of metabolic remobilization (phytol), and (2) as hydrophobic, surface-active protective molecules (terpenoid alcohols and long-chain fatty alcohols) localized in the integumentary structures and/or on leaf surfaces [<a href="#B113-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B113-molecules-31-00895">113</a>,<a href="#B114-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B114-molecules-31-00895">114</a>,<a href="#B115-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B115-molecules-31-00895">115</a>,<a href="#B116-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116-molecules-31-00895">116</a>].</p>
<p>This protective “logic” is particularly evident in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> and <em>P. natans</em>, where manool (16.44–19.61%) and accompanying labdane-type alcohols dominate. Labdane diterpenoids are well known for their antibacterial activity and may affect bacterial energy metabolism, including inhibition of ATP synthesis and hydrolysis [<a href="#B117-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B117-molecules-31-00895">117</a>].</p>
<p>In aquatic environments, the effective “radius of action” of hydrophobic alcohols is generally confined to the microscale, making the DBL a key factor. Periphyton has been shown to increase the thickness of this boundary layer and to modify pH and O<sub>2</sub> microprofiles at the leaf surface, while microsensor measurements reveal pronounced gradients of pH, O<sub>2</sub>, and redox potential around submerged leaves [<a href="#B19-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-molecules-31-00895">19</a>,<a href="#B118-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B118-molecules-31-00895">118</a>]. These conditions favor the local accumulation and retention of lipophilic terpenoid alcohols (including manool) and phytol, allowing them to act during the early stages of epiphyte and microbial colonization and to influence biofilm structure. The observed species specificity of epiphytic bacterial communities on macrophytes is consistent with the notion that the chemical “background” of plant surfaces acts as a selective factor, as demonstrated for <em>P. perfoliatus</em> and <em>M. spicatum</em> [<a href="#B119-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B119-molecules-31-00895">119</a>].</p>
<p>Among the major alcohols detected, manool deserves particular attention. Manool (a labdane-type diterpene alcohol) is a hydrophobic specialized plant metabolite that, in aquatic systems, most likely acts within contact interaction zones (macrophyte organ surfaces, epiphytic matrices, and detrital particles), where lipophilic terpenes can accumulate locally and affect biofilms [<a href="#B120-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B120-molecules-31-00895">120</a>]. Importantly for hydrophytes, manool has been identified as a common low-molecular-weight metabolite in <em>P. natans</em>, rendering it ecologically relevant for freshwater communities [<a href="#B97-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B97-molecules-31-00895">97</a>]. Available data indicate multi-level biological activity: antibacterial effects have been demonstrated against panels of clinically relevant strains, including mastitis-associated bacteria [<a href="#B117-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B117-molecules-31-00895">117</a>,<a href="#B121-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B121-molecules-31-00895">121</a>]. Manool has also been proposed as a promising agent for the treatment of periodontal infections due to its antibacterial properties [<a href="#B122-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B122-molecules-31-00895">122</a>]. Moreover, the antibacterial efficacy of manool, together with sclareol (a major component in <em>P. natans</em>), has been demonstrated by [<a href="#B123-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B123-molecules-31-00895">123</a>]. Phytochemical studies of <em>P. natans</em> and <em>Ruppia maritima</em> L. further suggest that high concentrations of biologically active ent-labdane diterpenes in these plants can affect other aquatic organisms and may exert anti-algal effects, thereby contributing to the maintenance of balance in aquatic systems [<a href="#B56-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-molecules-31-00895">56</a>,<a href="#B124-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B124-molecules-31-00895">124</a>,<a href="#B125-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B125-molecules-31-00895">125</a>]. Overall, in aquatic ecosystems, the ecological and biochemical roles of manool and sclareol appear to be primarily associated with antibacterial and anti-algal effects, particularly within biofilms and periphyton.</p>
<p>Another important compound is phytol, an isoprenoid (diterpene) alcohol that forms the phytyl side chain of chlorophylls <em>a</em> and <em>b</em> in living plant and algal cells and is therefore directly linked to the turnover of the photosynthetic apparatus in aquatic macrophytes, periphyton, and phytoplankton. In aquatic ecosystems, where phototrophs frequently experience fluctuations in light availability, temperature, and nutrient supply, chlorophyll degradation and phytol release are enhanced during periods of photostress. Recent studies consider the “fate of phytol” as a regulated module connecting chlorophyll catabolism with prenyl metabolism and antioxidant defense [<a href="#B116-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116-molecules-31-00895">116</a>].</p>
<p>Phytol was the leading alcohol in most of the studied species (up to 16.39% in <em>M. spicatum</em> and 13.05% in <em>S. emersum</em>) (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>), which is biochemically consistent with its origin from chlorophyll and with known pathways of phytol remobilization for tocopherol (vitamin E) synthesis and adaptation to oxidative stress [<a href="#B116-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B116-molecules-31-00895">116</a>]. In addition, phytol exhibits a broad spectrum of antibacterial and antifungal activities, and its mechanism of action has been elucidated, including induction of oxidative cell death in <em>Pseudomonas aeruginosa</em> [<a href="#B126-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B126-molecules-31-00895">126</a>,<a href="#B127-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B127-molecules-31-00895">127</a>].</p>
<p>Within the “macrophytes–periphyton–phytoplankton” framework, these findings support a working hypothesis of a local influence of phytol and lipophilic chlorophyll degradation products on the early stages of biofilm formation and on the composition of surface-associated microbial communities. Phytol has also been shown to exert protective or deterrent effects against aquatic insects and herbivorous larvae [<a href="#B128-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B128-molecules-31-00895">128</a>]. Furthermore, higher phytol concentrations combined with the presence of linoleic acid in the leaves of <em>Nuphar lutea</em> have been reported to reduce feeding by <em>Galerucella nymphaeae</em> L. [<a href="#B129-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B129-molecules-31-00895">129</a>], a typical consumer of <em>N. lutea</em> and <em>Nymphaea candida</em> J. et C. Presl. [<a href="#B130-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B130-molecules-31-00895">130</a>]. Collectively, these data suggest that phytol should be regarded not merely as a by-product of chlorophyll metabolism, but as an important component of plant chemical defense and resistance to pathogens and microbial colonization of plant surfaces.</p>
<p>In addition to terpenoid and chlorophyll-derived alcohols, long-chain fatty alcohols (primary <em>n</em>-alcohols, predominantly C14–C26+)—typical constituents of waxes and surface lipid coatings—were present among major LMWOCs in several species. In the studied macrophytes, these included, for example, tetradecan-1-ol (C14:0) in <em>M. spicatum</em> (3.70%); hexadecan-1-ol (C16:0) in <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, <em>P. natans</em>, and <em>P. pectinatus</em> (1.61–4.47%); heptadecan-1-ol (C17:0) in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (4.37%); octadecan-1-ol (C18:0) in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (1.33%); <em>(Z)</em>-icos-9-en-1-ol (C20:1) in <em>S. gramineum</em> (2.23%); and, among even longer homologues, pentacosan-1-ol (C25:0) and hexacosan-1-ol (C26:0) in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (2.27% and 2.28%, respectively) (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>).</p>
<p>The most plausible ecological and biochemical functions of these compounds in aquatic habitats are associated with the formation of a hydrophobic barrier interface, modulation of the immediate microenvironment through changes in surface physicochemical properties that may affect adhesion and early establishment of microbial biofilms and fouling communities, and indirect defense via a “physicochemical filter” that reduces vulnerability to biotic stressors. These interpretations are well supported by contemporary reviews on wax composition and biosynthesis and on the roles of fatty alcohols in forming protective surface layers in plants [<a href="#B52-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-molecules-31-00895">52</a>,<a href="#B53-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-molecules-31-00895">53</a>,<a href="#B131-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B131-molecules-31-00895">131</a>,<a href="#B132-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B132-molecules-31-00895">132</a>]. For one long-chain fatty alcohol, docosanol (C22:0), substantial antibiofilm and antivirulence activity against bacteria has been demonstrated [<a href="#B133-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B133-molecules-31-00895">133</a>], suggesting a potential capacity of long-chain fatty alcohols to influence biofilm formation processes in macrophytes; however, direct evidence for aquatic plants is currently lacking.</p>
<p>Finally, C6 alcohols of the GLVs pool should be specifically mentioned. These compounds reached major component levels only in representatives of the genus <em>Potamogeton</em>: <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (hexan-2-ol, 1.04%, and (<em>E</em>)-hex-2-en-1-ol, 1.93%) and <em>P. pectinatus</em> ((<em>E</em>)-hex-2-en-1-ol, 7.71%) (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). In addition, other stress-associated volatile alcohols and alcohol-like compounds of different biosynthetic origin—such as the terpenoid alcohol geranyllinalool and the apocarotenoid β-ionol—were detected in comparable concentrations in <em>Potamogeton</em> species (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). GLVs are closely linked to tissue damage and oxidative stress and function as rapid stress-induced signals involved in inter-organismal communication [<a href="#B134-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B134-molecules-31-00895">134</a>,<a href="#B135-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B135-molecules-31-00895">135</a>,<a href="#B136-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B136-molecules-31-00895">136</a>]. Multiple protective roles have been demonstrated for GLVs in plants, including both direct defensive effects and signal-mediated modulation of defense responses, including antipathogenic activity [<a href="#B73-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-molecules-31-00895">73</a>,<a href="#B136-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B136-molecules-31-00895">136</a>,<a href="#B137-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B137-molecules-31-00895">137</a>]. It is therefore likely that, in aquatic environments, such low-molecular-weight volatile alcohols and functionally related stress-associated compounds can act and retain ecological relevance within the near-surface DBL surrounding macrophyte surfaces.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot3-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.3. Hydrocarbons</h4>
<p>Among the major hydrocarbons identified, three n-alkanes showed the highest maximum relative contributions: tricosane (C23), pentacosane (C25), and heptacosane (C27) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>). Moreover, in <em>M. spicatum</em>, <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum, S. gramineum</em>, and <em>S. emersum</em>, these compounds were also included in the top three most abundant major LMWOCs by relative contribution (%) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/molecules-31-00895-t005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn4"><p>Note: –: the compound is not included in the major components of the species.</p></div></div></section><p>Data reported in [<a href="#B138-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B138-molecules-31-00895">138</a>] indicate that the same n-alkanes also ranked among the three most abundant in submerged macrophytes, whereas in emergent plants they occupied the 2nd (heptacosane, C27), 3rd (pentacosane, C25), and 4th (tricosane, C23) positions.</p>
<p>Long-chain aliphatic hydrocarbons (primarily n-alkanes) play a pivotal role in the formation of cuticular waxes in aquatic macrophytes and generate a cuticular–waxy “first-contact layer” at the leaf/shoot–water interface, thereby affecting surface wettability [<a href="#B139-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B139-molecules-31-00895">139</a>,<a href="#B140-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B140-molecules-31-00895">140</a>]. In aquatic habitats, their ecological function is expressed mainly as a structural protective module that influences surface wettability, the DBL, and the likelihood of initial attachment of epiphytes and microorganisms, thus mediating plant interactions with periphyton, the surface microbiome, and phytoplankton [<a href="#B113-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B113-molecules-31-00895">113</a>,<a href="#B141-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B141-molecules-31-00895">141</a>,<a href="#B142-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B142-molecules-31-00895">142</a>,<a href="#B143-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B143-molecules-31-00895">143</a>]. Our results reveal a pronounced dominance signal of mid-chain n-alkanes (C23–C27), which is widely used to diagnose the contribution of submerged and floating macrophytes to organic matter pools in aquatic systems, in contrast to emergent aquatic plants and terrestrial vegetation [<a href="#B138-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B138-molecules-31-00895">138</a>,<a href="#B144-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B144-molecules-31-00895">144</a>]. This pattern is most evident in <em>Sparganium</em> species, where C23, C25, and C27 dominate (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>), consistent with an enhanced wax-barrier function [<a href="#B138-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B138-molecules-31-00895">138</a>,<a href="#B139-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B139-molecules-31-00895">139</a>]. In <em>M. spicatum</em> and <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, the representation of this structural-protective hydrocarbon block is somewhat lower than in <em>Sparganium</em> (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>; <a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>), yet it remains substantial. This may suggest that, in species exhibiting high maximum hydrocarbon levels, a strategy of mitigating epiphyte-induced shading, maintaining tissue light regimes, and supporting photosynthetic performance could potentially be effective. By reducing “film-like” epiphytic pressure (shading plus competition for dissolved resources at the surface), macrophyte competitiveness increases and may indirectly contribute to the stability of the clear-water state in shallow aquatic ecosystems.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot4-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.4. Aldehydes</h4>
<p>In the metabolomic profiles of the 11 investigated macrophyte species (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>), major aldehydes were represented predominantly by lipid-derived compounds and exhibited strong species specificity. The major aldehydes included (i) GLVs/C6 aldehydes (primarily hexanal and (<em>E</em>)-hex-2-enal), (ii) medium- and long-chain aliphatic aldehydes (C9–C18; nonanal, pentadecanal, octadecanal, etc.), and (iii) the aromatic aldehydes benzaldehyde and 2-phenylacetaldehyde. The most distinct GLVs profile was observed in <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, where (<em>E</em>)-hex-2-enal reached 10.86%. Hexanal showed high relative abundances in several species and peaked at 5.08% in <em>C. demersum</em>. Among C9 aldehydes, nonanal was prominent (up to 7.58% in <em>S. gramineum</em>), whereas among C18 aldehydes, octadecanal reached 3.40% in <em>S. gramineum</em>. The long-chain aldehyde pentadecanal, detected in eight species, was particularly abundant in <em>M. spicatum</em> (5.82%), whereas in <em>N. lutea</em> it combined a moderate relative contribution (1.00%) with a high absolute concentration (74.28 μg·g<sup>−1</sup>), indicating a potentially substantial hydrophobic pool in tissues/surface-associated lipids. Aromatic aldehydes (benzaldehyde and 2-phenylacetaldehyde), as major components, were in our dataset primarily characteristic of the genus <em>Potamogeton</em>, peaking in <em>P. pectinatus</em> (benzaldehyde 2.27%; 2-phenylacetaldehyde 1.68%), whereas in <em>P. natans</em> they were not among major LMWOCs. Benzaldehyde as a major component was also recorded in <em>C. demersum</em> (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>).</p>
<p>The particularly pronounced GLVs-type C6 aldehydes in <em>Potamogeton</em> species (together with other GLVs) (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>) are characteristic of oxylipin-related chemistry and are consistent with the role of these compounds in rapid defensive responses. Direct antimicrobial activity and membrane-targeting effects have been reported for GLVs aldehydes, supporting their contribution to antifouling and the “chemical hygiene” of shoot surfaces in the periphyton zone [<a href="#B73-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-molecules-31-00895">73</a>,<a href="#B145-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B145-molecules-31-00895">145</a>,<a href="#B146-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B146-molecules-31-00895">146</a>].</p>
<p>Among the six GLVs considered, (3<em>E</em>)-hexenal ((<em>E</em>)-hex-3-enal) was reported as the most effective compound, showing a prominent bacteriostatic effect [<a href="#B145-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B145-molecules-31-00895">145</a>]. The major LMWOCs (<em>E</em>)-hex-2-enal detected in <em>P. amphibia</em> and <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>) likely fulfill similar functions.</p>
<p>Second, in other taxa, C9–C18 aldehydes (nonanal, pentadecanal, octadecanal) predominate, plausibly reflecting an oxidative lipid/surface-wax component and potentially constraining the development of microbial biofilms [<a href="#B146-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B146-molecules-31-00895">146</a>,<a href="#B147-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B147-molecules-31-00895">147</a>,<a href="#B148-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B148-molecules-31-00895">148</a>], which classifies them as components of antifouling and surface protection in aquatic plants.</p>
<p>Aromatic aldehydes (benzaldehyde, 2-phenylacetaldehyde) may strengthen antibacterial and, potentially, algastatic pressure within the leaf boundary layer [<a href="#B149-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B149-molecules-31-00895">149</a>,<a href="#B150-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B150-molecules-31-00895">150</a>].</p>
<p>Thus, aldehydes constitute a reactive and “fast” fraction of the LMWM of aquatic macrophytes, linking plant stress physiology to community-level effects. At the ecosystem scale, this aligns with current concepts that allelopathy and chemical defense by aquatic macrophytes can reduce phytoplankton and/or epiphytes in shallow-water ecosystems, improve the light climate within plant stands, and promote the resilience of a clear-water state [<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B11-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-molecules-31-00895">11</a>,<a href="#B151-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-molecules-31-00895">151</a>,<a href="#B152-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B152-molecules-31-00895">152</a>,<a href="#B153-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B153-molecules-31-00895">153</a>], while aldehydes most likely act as part of a multicomponent chemical “cocktail” [<a href="#B102-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B102-molecules-31-00895">102</a>,<a href="#B103-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B103-molecules-31-00895">103</a>,<a href="#B104-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B104-molecules-31-00895">104</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot5-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.5. Esters</h4>
<p>In the present study, within this group, we considered a set of compounds dominated by esters, as well as furans and other lipophilic oxygenated constituents assigned to this group under the adopted GC–MS classification.</p>
<p>Within the lipophilic “Esters” fraction, three main subsets can be distinguished: alkyl esters of fatty acids, terpenoid carboxylates, and furan-type oxygenates (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). Distinct interspecific contrasts were observed, which may be relevant for the chemical and functional structuring of the plant–water boundary layer. <em>P. natans</em> was most distinctive, with a strong dominance of the terpenoid carboxylates (methyl athecate (11.21%) and methyl sandaracopimarate (10.56%)), accompanied by ethyl octadeca-9,12-dienoate (2.50%). In <em>C. demersum</em> and several <em>Potamogeton</em> taxa, the profile shifted towards fatty-acid alkyl esters: methyl octadecanoate reached 6.28% in <em>C. demersum</em>, 4.54% in <em>P. pectinatus</em>, and 2.69% in <em>P. perfoliatus</em>. <em>M. spicatum</em> exhibited elevated maximum relative abundances of 3,7,11,15-tetramethylhexadec-2-enyl acetate (4.11%) along with increased furans (2-pentylfuran, 2.63%; 2-[(<em>Z</em>)-pent-2-enyl]furan, 1.6%), whereas in <em>N. lutea</em> and <em>P. amphibia</em> this fraction was largely furan-driven (~1.0–1.3%). In <em>Sparganium</em> spp. and <em>L. dortmanna</em>, “Esters” compounds did not reach the major-component threshold (&gt;1%).</p>
<p>It has been shown that a range of lipophilic esters can be adsorbed/accumulated in natural biofilms [<a href="#B154-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B154-molecules-31-00895">154</a>]. By analogy, this suggests that ester-type metabolites produced by macrophytes may concentrate within epiphytic biofilms and modify their properties (e.g., adhesion and community composition), potentially affecting epiphyte load and, indirectly, the light regime.</p>
<p>Fatty-acid methyl esters (FAMEs) from marine microalgae exhibit antimicrobial activity against various Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria; their mode of action is linked to membrane damage and leakage of intracellular contents [<a href="#B155-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B155-molecules-31-00895">155</a>]. Moreover, these FAMEs significantly reduced the expression of genes associated with quorum sensing and biofilm formation [<a href="#B151-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-molecules-31-00895">151</a>,<a href="#B155-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B155-molecules-31-00895">155</a>]. Vijay et al. [<a href="#B156-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B156-molecules-31-00895">156</a>] further reported antibacterial, antivirulence, and antibiofilm activities of fatty-acid methyl esters.</p>
<p>Taken together, these observations support the hypothesis that ester-type components in macrophytes may modulate epiphytic communities and periphyton properties and, through changes in biofouling and light conditions, indirectly influence phytoplankton and ecosystem-level outcomes. However, this requires direct experimental validation.</p>
<p>The predominance of fatty-acid alkyl esters in <em>C. demersum</em> and <em>Potamogeton</em> species. may reflect membrane-active control of early biofilm colonizers through antibacterial and antifungal activities, including at low minimum inhibitory concentrations [<a href="#B157-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B157-molecules-31-00895">157</a>,<a href="#B158-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B158-molecules-31-00895">158</a>].</p>
<p>Finally, 2-pentylfuran has been described as a bioactive volatile metabolite in microbe–plant systems capable of stimulating the growth of the model plant <em>Arabidopsis thaliana</em> [<a href="#B159-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B159-molecules-31-00895">159</a>]. Its occurrence as a major constituent in six of the eleven studied macrophytes suggests that furans may act as potential signal-modulating components in aquatic macrophytes and within the epiphytic biofilm microzone; nevertheless, direct evidence for such roles in aquatic systems is still lacking.</p>
<p>Given that ester-type LMWOCs in aquatic macrophytes remain poorly characterized, the direction and magnitude of their effects are likely context-dependent (ester structure, concentration, and community composition). Therefore, any prediction for epiphyton in specific situations should be considered a species-specific hypothesis.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot6-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.6. Ketones</h4>
<p>Ketones occurred as major components in 9 out of 11 macrophyte species. Two dominant blocks were identified: (i) apocarotenoid/ionone-like ketones and (ii) the isoprenoid ketone 6,10,14-trimethylpentadecan-2-one (phytone). The highest relative abundances of β-ionone were found in <em>C. demersum</em> (6.74%) and <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (5.28%), whereas phytone reached 9.09% in <em>P. pectinatus</em> and remained at 4.57–5.23% in <em>M. spicatum</em>, <em>P. amphibia</em>, and <em>C. demersum</em> (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>).</p>
<p>Among all ketones, only β-ionone entered the Top 3 most abundant major LMWOCs (in <em>C. demersum</em>) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>). A distinctive signal was the pronounced dominance of the steroid-like ketone androst-4-ene-3,6,17-trione in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (13.13%). No major ketones were detected in <em>N. lutea</em> and <em>S. gramineum</em>. <em>L. dortmanna</em> contained the cyclic ketone cyclohexadec-8-en-1-one (4.75%).</p>
<p>Aquatic macrophytes have been shown to emit biogenic volatile organic compounds (BVOCs), including ketones [<a href="#B153-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B153-molecules-31-00895">153</a>], and in aquatic environments BVOCs (including apocarotenoid/ionone-like ketones) may function as chemical messengers and/or allelopathic signals affecting other phototrophs [<a href="#B160-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B160-molecules-31-00895">160</a>]. Ketones are therefore considered to have substantial allelochemical potential [<a href="#B160-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B160-molecules-31-00895">160</a>].</p>
<p>β-Ionone is a proven effective allelochemical and plant defense element: it has been experimentally shown to disrupt the photosynthetic system of <em>Microcystis aeruginosa</em> (including PSII-related targets), which is consistent with anti-cyanobacterial pressure exerted by macrophytes in waterbodies [<a href="#B151-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-molecules-31-00895">151</a>,<a href="#B161-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B161-molecules-31-00895">161</a>,<a href="#B162-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B162-molecules-31-00895">162</a>]. It has also been shown that β-ionone and other apocarotenoids comprise flavors, aromas, pigments, growth regulators, and defense compounds; serve as ecological cues; act as insect attractants or repellents; and display antibacterial and fungicidal properties [<a href="#B162-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B162-molecules-31-00895">162</a>,<a href="#B163-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B163-molecules-31-00895">163</a>]. β-Ionone is listed among major allelopathic agents that can induce strong inhibitory effects on phototrophs, up to lethal outcomes, by triggering programmed cell death [<a href="#B160-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B160-molecules-31-00895">160</a>,<a href="#B164-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B164-molecules-31-00895">164</a>].</p>
<p>It has been established that β-ionone and geranylacetone (identified as a major component in <em>P. perfoliatus</em>) are synthesized and released into the environment by red and green algae, presumably to control surrounding organisms in allelopathic interactions [<a href="#B165-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B165-molecules-31-00895">165</a>,<a href="#B166-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B166-molecules-31-00895">166</a>]. At the ecosystem level, such properties of apocarotenoids may contribute to reduced phytoplankton dominance and to the maintenance of a macrophyte-dominated state in waterbodies [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B60-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-molecules-31-00895">60</a>].</p>
<p>Phytone is a widespread BVOCs compound not only among plants but also among insects [<a href="#B167-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B167-molecules-31-00895">167</a>,<a href="#B168-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B168-molecules-31-00895">168</a>]. It exhibits allelopathic, antimicrobial, antifungal, and pheromonal activity [<a href="#B169-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B169-molecules-31-00895">169</a>,<a href="#B170-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B170-molecules-31-00895">170</a>,<a href="#B171-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B171-molecules-31-00895">171</a>]. Phytone is interpreted as a product/marker of photo- and oxidative processing of phytol chains (chlorophyll degradation), reflecting the intensity of light/oxidative stress and simultaneously having the potential to influence the microbiome/periphyton via BVOCs-mediated interactions [<a href="#B151-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-molecules-31-00895">151</a>,<a href="#B162-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B162-molecules-31-00895">162</a>,<a href="#B172-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B172-molecules-31-00895">172</a>].</p>
<p>For ketoisophorone, which is a major component in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> and <em>P. pectinatus</em> (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>), the literature indicates multiple ecological functions. First, essential oils in which ketoisophorone occurs as a major or minor component exhibit conspicuous phytotoxic and allelopathic properties [<a href="#B173-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B173-molecules-31-00895">173</a>,<a href="#B174-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B174-molecules-31-00895">174</a>]. Second, this ketone is relevant to pollination ecology in plants and acts as a pheromone in insect mating behavior [<a href="#B175-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B175-molecules-31-00895">175</a>,<a href="#B176-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B176-molecules-31-00895">176</a>].</p>
<p>In aquatic ecosystems, ketoisophorone (4-oxoisophorone), together with other ketones, is regularly reported among the volatile organic compounds (VOCs) of aquatic phototrophs (micro- and macrophytes), and in some cases it has been described as a major ketone [<a href="#B27-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-molecules-31-00895">27</a>,<a href="#B95-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B95-molecules-31-00895">95</a>,<a href="#B177-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B177-molecules-31-00895">177</a>,<a href="#B178-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B178-molecules-31-00895">178</a>,<a href="#B179-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B179-molecules-31-00895">179</a>]. These observations are consistent with the possibility that, in water, ketoisophorone may serve as a marker of physiological status and stress-associated restructuring of macrophyte VOCs profiles and may participate in allelopathic interactions at the levels of the microbiome, periphyton, and phytoplankton.</p>
<p>Along with “classical” GLVs, which by definition are mainly C6 aldehydes, C6 alcohols, and their acyl esters [<a href="#B136-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B136-molecules-31-00895">136</a>], our dataset revealed low-molecular-weight volatile ketones as major LMWOCs—hexan-2-one, hexan-3-one, heptan-2-one, oct-7-en-2-one, and others (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). These compounds are better interpreted not as GLVs sensu stricto but as GLVs-like VOCs [<a href="#B136-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B136-molecules-31-00895">136</a>]. Notably, in our material, “green” alcohols (GLVs) reached major-compound levels only in representatives of <em>Potamogeton</em>, whereas ketone major VOCs were characteristic of other taxa as well (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). In aquatic ecosystems, such VOCs are considered potentially important infochemicals and markers of physiological status [<a href="#B151-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-molecules-31-00895">151</a>]. An additional argument for their natural relevance is that, in a freshwater enrichment culture “algae–bacteria”, hexan-2-one and hexan-3-one were directly detected as exometabolites with a possible allelopathic role [<a href="#B180-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B180-molecules-31-00895">180</a>]. Defensive, repellent, and antibacterial properties have been described for 2-heptanone and its derivatives, including in red algae [<a href="#B181-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B181-molecules-31-00895">181</a>,<a href="#B182-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B182-molecules-31-00895">182</a>,<a href="#B183-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B183-molecules-31-00895">183</a>].</p>
<p>More generally, experimental evidence indicates that odor-active VOCs blends of cyanobacterial biofilms, including ketones, can serve as chemical cues in aquatic environments (demonstrated using nematodes), i.e., VOCs have real ecological functions in micro- and periphyton habitats [<a href="#B184-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B184-molecules-31-00895">184</a>].</p>
<p>Finally, the identified major occurrence of androst-4-ene-3,6,17-trione in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> requires careful analysis and verification. However, the occurrence/conversion of progestogens/androgens in plants, the involvement of aquatic plants in the fate of hormone-like micropollutants, and active microbial degradation of steroids make its contribution to biofilm restructuring and cascading effects on periphyton and phytoplankton ecologically plausible [<a href="#B185-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B185-molecules-31-00895">185</a>,<a href="#B186-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B186-molecules-31-00895">186</a>].</p>
<p>Overall, ionone-like and isoprenoid ketone profiles in aquatic macrophytes provide a basis for chemically mediated pressure on phototrophic and microbial compartments (periphyton/phytoplankton), which—other factors being equal—may shift aquatic systems toward a more “macrophyte” state.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot7-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.7. Aromatic Hydrocarbons</h4>
<p>Only two aromatic hydrocarbons (AH) reached the level of major compounds in the macrophytes studied: 1,2-xylene (o-xylene) was detected in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> with a maximum relative abundance of 1.12%, and 1-methyl-7-(propan-2-yl)phenanthrene (retene) was detected in <em>P. amphibia</em> with a maximum relative abundance of 1.00% (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>). In the remaining studied species, aromatic hydrocarbons were not recorded as major compounds.</p>
<p>It is important to note that, for AH, the duality of possible origin is critical: detection in plant samples may reflect (i) a plant-associated LMWOCs pool [<a href="#B80-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-molecules-31-00895">80</a>], but also (ii) sorption/re-emission from water and bottom sediments, inputs from the catchment/atmosphere, and anthropogenic sources [<a href="#B187-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B187-molecules-31-00895">187</a>,<a href="#B188-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B188-molecules-31-00895">188</a>,<a href="#B189-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B189-molecules-31-00895">189</a>,<a href="#B190-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B190-molecules-31-00895">190</a>]. It has been estimated that the global potential of secondary organic aerosol formation from biogenic plant benzenoids is approximately comparable to that from anthropogenic benzenoids [<a href="#B191-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B191-molecules-31-00895">191</a>]. Experimentally, inhibition of the freshwater green alga <em>Selenastrum capricornutum</em> by volatile aromatic hydrocarbons (including xylene components in mixtures), with possible additive and synergistic effects, has been demonstrated [<a href="#B192-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B192-molecules-31-00895">192</a>], which is consistent with the possibility of local “chemical pressure” within the boundary layer of macrophyte stands.</p>
<p>Retene may originate from vascular plants, as well as possibly from algae and cyanobacteria [<a href="#B193-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B193-molecules-31-00895">193</a>,<a href="#B194-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B194-molecules-31-00895">194</a>]. Retene can also be regarded as a pyrogenic/terrigenous PAH marker associated with inputs from wood combustion and/or catchment flows, as well as with potential accumulation in areas impacted by the pulp and paper industry [<a href="#B195-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B195-molecules-31-00895">195</a>]. This is particularly relevant for Lake Ladoga, where our <em>P. perfoliatus</em> samples were collected in different habitats and whose catchment hosted active pulp and paper mills [<a href="#B196-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B196-molecules-31-00895">196</a>].</p>
<p>Given these considerations, interpreting o-xylene and retene as major “plant metabolites” is possible, but it requires caution and rigorous experimental verification, while their biological effects on aquatic organisms remain relevant regardless of origin [<a href="#B151-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B151-molecules-31-00895">151</a>,<a href="#B189-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B189-molecules-31-00895">189</a>].</p>
<p>Thus, conceptually, a biogenically associated contribution of AH from vegetation in general to processes in aquatic ecosystems is plausible [<a href="#B191-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B191-molecules-31-00895">191</a>]; however, the mere occurrence of o-xylene and retene as major components of the LMWM in <em>P. perfoliatus</em> and <em>P. amphibia</em> does not constitute evidence of their purposeful biosynthesis and allelopathy.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot8-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.8. Diverse Functional Groups</h4>
<p>In the “Diverse functional groups” class, based on maximum relative abundances (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>), the most pronounced contribution was observed in <em>Potamogeton</em> species, whereas in other taxa (<em>M. spicatum</em>, <em>Sparganium</em> spp., <em>N. lutea</em>, <em>P. amphibia</em>, <em>L. dortmanna</em>) the contents of LMWOCs assigned to this class were below the threshold for major components (&lt;1% of total LMWOCs). In <em>P. natans</em>, the highest value was recorded for a steroidal acetate (10.19%), and relatively high values were also observed for polyalthic acid (6.31%). In <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, high proportions were found for cyclopenta[a]phenanthrene-type derivatives with retention index (RI) = 2244 (pregnanolone-like steroid; tentative library ID, 7.59%) and RI = 2467 (tentative library ID, 5.20%). In <em>P. pectinatus</em>, the leading component was sandaracopimarinol (7.35%), accompanied by diterpene esters (methyl daniellate 2.10%<strong>,</strong> methyl lambertianate 1.41%). In <em>C. demersum</em>, a moderate contribution of a pregnanolone-like steroid (tentative library ID, 1.67%) was observed (<a href="#app1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials, Table S1</a>).</p>
<p>The ability of plants to carry out biotransformations of pregnane steroids and to form pregnanolone-like derivatives is supported by experimental evidence and direct findings in specific plant objects [<a href="#B197-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B197-molecules-31-00895">197</a>,<a href="#B198-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B198-molecules-31-00895">198</a>,<a href="#B199-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B199-molecules-31-00895">199</a>,<a href="#B200-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B200-molecules-31-00895">200</a>]. However, under GC–MS library identification without confirmation by an authentic standard, the stereochemistry of steroids is generally not verified; therefore, the steroid peaks reported above are more appropriately interpreted as pregnanolone-like (tentative ID).</p>
<p>It should also be noted that steroid derivatives in general (including ecdysteroids in some plants) may contribute to antifeedant defense and can function as molting and metamorphosis hormones in crustaceans and insects when they consume plants [<a href="#B80-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-molecules-31-00895">80</a>,<a href="#B201-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B201-molecules-31-00895">201</a>,<a href="#B202-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B202-molecules-31-00895">202</a>,<a href="#B203-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B203-molecules-31-00895">203</a>].</p>
<p>Occurrences of sandaracopimarinol, a typical resin-derived diterpene alcohol, have been documented mainly in coniferous/cypress taxa (<em>Tetraclinis articulata</em>, <em>Cryptomeria japonica</em>, <em>Pinus nigra</em>) [<a href="#B204-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B204-molecules-31-00895">204</a>,<a href="#B205-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B205-molecules-31-00895">205</a>,<a href="#B206-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B206-molecules-31-00895">206</a>,<a href="#B207-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B207-molecules-31-00895">207</a>]. This compound, within a hexane heartwood extract, was shown to inhibit the growth of the cyanobacterium <em>Microcystis aeruginosa</em> [<a href="#B205-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B205-molecules-31-00895">205</a>].</p>
<p>Polyalthic acid (a furanolabdane diterpene/labdane-type diterpenoid acid) is reliably described in plants as an isolated and structurally characterized natural product; its stereochemistry was established in a classical study on <em>Polyalthia fragrans</em> (Annonaceae) [<a href="#B208-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B208-molecules-31-00895">208</a>]. This compound has also been isolated from <em>Vitex rotundifolia</em> (Lamiaceae), described as a “major stable compound” in <em>Daniella oliveri</em> (Fabaceae), and characterized as bioactive [<a href="#B209-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B209-molecules-31-00895">209</a>,<a href="#B210-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B210-molecules-31-00895">210</a>].</p>
<p>The potential allelopathic relevance of compounds within the “Diverse functional groups” class appears to be particularly pertinent to Potamogetonaceae. Specifically, ent-labdane diterpenes have demonstrated effects on algae (<em>Selenastrum capricornutum</em>) and other aquatic organisms [<a href="#B125-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B125-molecules-31-00895">125</a>], and specific antialgal diterpenes/lactones have been described from <em>P. natans</em> [<a href="#B56-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-molecules-31-00895">56</a>,<a href="#B211-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B211-molecules-31-00895">211</a>]. Accordingly, in <em>P. natans</em>, the combination of a relatively high share of polyalthic acid (6.31%) with abundant steroidal esters (10.19%) implies a potentially substantial chemically mediated constraint on the microphyte component (phytoplankton/epiphytic algae) and/or the bacterial phase of biofilms. This is consistent with the reported broad antimicrobial profile of polyalthic acid [<a href="#B212-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B212-molecules-31-00895">212</a>,<a href="#B213-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B213-molecules-31-00895">213</a>].</p>
<p>For <em>P. pectinatus</em>, the possible presence of sandaracopimarinol with a high contribution to the LMWM (7.35%), together with reports of ent-labdanes in this species showing strong algicidal activity against <em>Raphidocelis subcapitata</em> [<a href="#B214-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B214-molecules-31-00895">214</a>,<a href="#B215-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B215-molecules-31-00895">215</a>], supports the hypothesis that a complex of lipophilic terpene compounds may influence periphyton communities. For <em>P. perfoliatus</em>, the high contribution of steroid derivatives (7.59% and 5.20%; tentative IDs) may plausibly reflect both stress adaptation and involvement in regulating the microenvironment near plant surfaces (leaves, stems).</p>
<p>Lipophilic steroid–terpenoid components are most functionally relevant at the plant surface–water interface, where they can potentially affect biofilm/periphyton development and, indirectly, competitive interactions with phytoplankton [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B20-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-molecules-31-00895">20</a>]. Overall, the present data support the hypothesis that <em>Potamogeton</em>-dominated stands, as well as stands formed by other macrophytes, may contribute to clear-water states via chemically mediated weakening of periphyton and/or phytoplankton [<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>,<a href="#B56-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-molecules-31-00895">56</a>,<a href="#B125-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B125-molecules-31-00895">125</a>,<a href="#B211-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B211-molecules-31-00895">211</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot3dot9-molecules-31-00895"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3.9. Sulfur-Containing LMWOCs</h4>
<p>The last group in which major compounds were detected was sulfur-containing LMWOCs. Within this “sulfur” group, GC–MS revealed three major compounds that were confined to a limited number of taxa. 1,3-benzothiazole was recorded in <em>P. amphibia</em> (2.09%) and <em>P. perfoliatus</em> (1.05%). Two lipophilic S-esters—pentadecyl propan-2-yl sulfite and octadecyl propan-2-yl sulfite—were found only in <em>Sparganium</em>: in <em>S.</em> × <em>foliosum</em> (1.59% and 2.19%, respectively) and in <em>S. gramineum</em>, where octadecyl propan-2-yl sulfite reached a maximum of 10.367%. In the LMWM of the remaining species, sulfur-containing components were not detected as major constituents.</p>
<p>1,3-Benzothiazole is considered a naturally occurring volatile and is a widespread constituent of essential oils in terrestrial plants, where it is primarily associated with signaling functions [<a href="#B216-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B216-molecules-31-00895">216</a>,<a href="#B217-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B217-molecules-31-00895">217</a>,<a href="#B218-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B218-molecules-31-00895">218</a>]. In a study on allelopathic exudates of two <em>Potamogeton</em> species [<a href="#B62-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-molecules-31-00895">62</a>], the authors explicitly reported benzothiazole (as well as 2-(methylthio)-benzothiazole) among compounds identified by GC–MS in the most active lipophilic fraction (CH<sub>2</sub>Cl<sub>2</sub> extract) of incubation water that inhibited <em>Selenastrum capricornutum</em> and a toxic strain of <em>Microcystis aeruginosa</em>. Importantly, these compounds were not detected in the culture-medium control, yet were present in treatments with macrophytes at some of the highest relative abundances. This pattern indicates a direct association with the macrophytes (<em>Potamogeton maackianus</em> and <em>Potamogeton malaianus</em>) and supports considering benzothiazole as a candidate compound involved in chemical interactions between macrophytes and microalgae/cyanobacteria (an allelopathic “candidate”). However, the relative contribution of plant metabolism versus microbial transformation requires additional verification (further experimental work). Despite this caveat, there are grounds to regard benzothiazole as an endogenous metabolite of freshwater macrophytes with potential signaling and allelopathic functions.</p>
<p>Regarding pentadecyl propan-2-yl sulfite and octadecyl propan-2-yl sulfite, there is evidence that these compounds occur in GC–MS profiles of essential oils and extracts from terrestrial plants, including <em>Isodon rugosus</em> (Lamiaceae) and <em>Gomphrena globosa</em> (Amaranthaceae) [<a href="#B219-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B219-molecules-31-00895">219</a>,<a href="#B220-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B220-molecules-31-00895">220</a>]. Moreover, octadecyl propan-2-yl sulfite from <em>G. globosa</em> has been reported to exhibit antibacterial activity [<a href="#B221-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B221-molecules-31-00895">221</a>].</p>
<p>Natural sulfur-containing plant metabolites have been reported to exhibit antimicrobial activity against various bacterial and fungal pathogens, making them promising candidates for antibiotic development [<a href="#B222-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B222-molecules-31-00895">222</a>]. Furthermore, many of them exhibit the ability to induce apoptosis and inhibit tumor cell proliferation [<a href="#B222-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B222-molecules-31-00895">222</a>].</p>
<p>It was noted that biogenic sulphides produced by charophytes may be most effective at the cell-surface boundary of these macroalgae [<a href="#B223-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B223-molecules-31-00895">223</a>]. The same author emphasized that, in general, little is known about the chemical ecology of biogenic sulfur compounds in freshwater systems [<a href="#B223-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B223-molecules-31-00895">223</a>]. At the same time, recent studies indicate that, in terrestrial plants, organic sulfur compounds can exert allelopathic as well as antibacterial activities [<a href="#B224-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B224-molecules-31-00895">224</a>,<a href="#B225-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B225-molecules-31-00895">225</a>,<a href="#B226-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B226-molecules-31-00895">226</a>]. For aquatic ecosystems, it has been suggested that such compounds most likely have signaling roles and may also act as allelochemicals [<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B93-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93-molecules-31-00895">93</a>,<a href="#B223-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B223-molecules-31-00895">223</a>].</p>
<p>Overall, the patterns identified for major LMWOCs across different chemical classes support the view that these compounds primarily exert ecologically meaningful effects within DBL-mediated microenvironments at the plant–water interface and on macrophyte surfaces, where small-scale gradients and frequent contact with epiphytes and the microbiome may enable functionally relevant metabolites to produce community-level effects. At the same time, allelopathic effects of macrophytes on phytoplankton at the scale of entire shallow-water ecosystems—together with other mechanisms that promote a ‘clear-water’ ecosystem state and that have been well documented across studies [<a href="#B1-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-molecules-31-00895">1</a>,<a href="#B2-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-molecules-31-00895">2</a>,<a href="#B3-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-molecules-31-00895">3</a>,<a href="#B4-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-molecules-31-00895">4</a>,<a href="#B7-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-molecules-31-00895">7</a>,<a href="#B9-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-molecules-31-00895">9</a>,<a href="#B10-molecules-31-00895" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-molecules-31-00895">10</a>]—also indicate the functional relevance of macrophyte metabolites beyond the DBL.</p></section></section></section><section id="sec3-molecules-31-00895"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-molecules-31-00895"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Plant Material</h3>
<p>In this study, we analyzed the major components of the LMWM of the following widely distributed freshwater macrophytes: <em>Myriophyllum spicatum</em> L., <em>Sparganium emersum</em> Rehm., <em>Sparganium gramineum</em> Georgi, the hybrid <em>Sparganium</em> × <em>foliosum</em> A. A. Bobrov, Volkova, Mochalova et Chemeris, <em>Persicaria amphibia</em> (L.) Delarbre, <em>Potamogeton perfoliatus</em> L., <em>Nuphar lutea</em> (L.) Sibth. &amp; Sm., <em>Potamogeton pectinatus</em> L., <em>Potamogeton natans</em> L., <em>Lobelia dortmanna</em> L., and <em>Ceratophyllum demersum</em> L.</p>
<p>Macrophyte samples were collected from diverse habitats in several water bodies: lakes in the Novgorod, Moscow, Tver, and Yaroslavl regions (Russia) (<em>S. emersum, S. gramineum, S.</em> × <em>foliosum, L. dortmanna</em>); lakes of the Karelian Isthmus, Northwestern Russia (<em>N. lutea, P. natans</em>, <em>M. spicatum</em>); Lake Ladoga (<em>P. perfoliatus, P. pectinatus, P. amphibia, M. spicatum</em>); floodplain lakes of the Volga–Akhtuba floodplain (Astrakhan Region, Russian Federation) (<em>P. pectinatus</em>, <em>C. demersum</em>); and Lake Naroch (Belarus) (<em>M. spicatum</em>) (<a href="#molecules-31-00895-t006" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>).</p>
